POST-P14i ✅ 2026-08-30 — **The commit graph refreshes itself when history moves.** Raised from the field: committing did not put the new commit in the graph, and the reporter noted the sort was already `by date`. **Real, and reproduced in the harness before a line was changed** — after a CLI commit the model's `headSha` was the new sha (watcher → refresher → model all fine) while the log session's row count sat unchanged for five seconds; `gittree.log.refresh` then took it to the expected count. The sort was a red herring: `sort` reaches git as `--date-order` vs `--topo-order` at *page-fetch* time (`logFilter.ts:57`), and no fetch was happening. **Root cause:** `LogViewProvider.startSession` did subscribe to `repo.model.onDidChange`, but wired it to `evtBranches` only — the Branches *pane* re-requested its refs and the commit rows were never re-paged. The graph's sole rebuild path is `evtReset`, raised only from `applyFilter`, whose ten call sites are all user acts; `refresh()` had exactly one caller, the toolbar command. So *every* history change went unseen — commit, amend, reset, checkout, merge, rebase, pull — whatever raised it, GitTree, the built-in Git extension or a terminal. Verified in the shipped `dist/extension.js` and `dist/webviews/log/index.js`, not just in source. **Not a deviation:** nothing in `docs/deviations.md` claimed manual-only refresh, and every other view in the tree already listens to the model (`changeBars.ts:114`, `branchesView.ts:47`, `worktrees.ts:428`, `statusBar.ts:79`, `commitWindowView.ts:98`) — the log was the outlier, so by our own rule this was a gap.

**The trigger is gated on history, never on `status`.** `refs` covers heads/remotes/tags, so it catches any commit that moved a branch; a commit on a detached HEAD moves no ref, so `headSha` is compared directly rather than trusting `sections.has('head')`, which also fires for ahead/behind and `lastFetchAt` and would re-page a `refs: 'HEAD'` graph after a fetch that changed nothing it draws. `status` fires on every save and every stage and must never re-page — one `git log` per keypress in a watched file — which is asserted directly rather than left to inspection. **Coalesced and deferred, because the read is not free:** §13 budgets page 0 at ≤600 ms on 100k commits, and a rebase moves `refs` once per step, so refreshes debounce at 300 ms and nothing runs at all while the panel is hidden — the pending flag is flushed from the existing `onDidChangeVisibility` handler, so a closed log costs zero git. The debounce also sidesteps a re-entrancy trap: `refresh()` synchronously disposes the `refsSub` that owns the very subscription the handler is running inside.

**A latent race had to be closed first, and it was the reason to look.** `sendPage` captured `session`, awaited the read, then pushed rows and notified unconditionally — with no re-check that the session was still current. The OperationQueue **cancels and requeues in-flight readers when a mutation arrives** (`operationQueue.ts:124`), so a page read started before a commit resolves *after* it, after the auto-refresh has already built the next session; the rows would grow a dead row store, advance a dead layout, and reach the webview as another `page: 0`, which it treats as a *replacement* — overwriting the fresh page with the stale one. Rare while refresh was a hand-paced button, routine the moment history re-pages on its own. Now guarded.

**Preserving the user's place is what makes the reset acceptable.** A plain `evtReset` clears rows, selection and details, empties the Changed Files pane with them, and — because emptying the list collapses `VirtualList`'s spacer and the browser then clamps `scrollTop` — throws the scroll away too. Rows genuinely must be rebuilt (a new commit renumbers every `--skip` page and relays the graph), so instead the host marks the reset `preserve` and the webview re-anchors: on the focused commit if there was one, otherwise on the row that was at the top — scroll only, because silently selecting a commit the user never picked would repaint both right-hand panes. **The restore moved out of the `evtRows` handler into an effect on the committed `rows`, and that was a real defect, not a tidy-up:** setting a scroll offset before React has painted the taller spacer is silently clamped, which made the scroll-anchor test fail 3 runs in 5 until it was found. Waiting for the committed rows also means an anchor that page 0 does not carry is simply found on whichever later page does. `VirtualList` grew an additive `align: 'top'` for it — `'auto'` from a freshly reset list (scrollTop 0) leaves the anchor at the *bottom* of the window, which is not "stay put".

**Fixed in passing, by construction:** stale ref chips. The page cache keys on `filterKey|page|headSha` only, so a ref that moved without HEAD moving — a fetch, a branch or tag created elsewhere — was served from cache with its old decorations; `refresh()` invalidates, so the chips now follow history for free.

Testing: **+3 Playwright** — a preserved reset keeps the focused commit and its details pane, a user-asked reset still clears the selection (the pre-existing contract, pinned so the new flag cannot quietly widen), and a preserved reset under *no* selection holds the scroll without inventing a selection; **+2 integration** on `phase04` — an empty commit re-pages the graph with no manual refresh and follows a `reset --hard` back down (asserted through a new `autoRefreshCount` seam so the re-page is attributed to the auto-refresh path, not to something else that happened to reset the session), and a status-only change leaves the count untouched past the debounce. The empty commit is deliberate: it moves `refs/heads/*` without touching the worktree, so the restore cannot destroy anything the test did not create.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **788 unit (unchanged) · 209 webview (+3, 3 skipped) · 261 integration passing + 2 pending, 1 failing**. **The one failure is P7-T2 at 549 ms against its 500 ms bound** — the stash-latency flake this log has been recording since POST-P14f and which is already an open owner decision; the *baseline* run of the same suite on the unmodified tree under the same machine load failed it too (515 ms) plus P1-T7 on a 30 s `waitFor`, so the tree was not green before this change either. Earlier full runs of this change were dirtier still (8, then 5, then 2 failures) on a machine also running four unrelated vite dev servers; the one failure that landed close enough to be suspicious, P12-T5 `a two-path filter pages exactly git log -- a b`, was chased rather than written off — it passes 23/23 with P12 in isolation and passed in the final full run, and the baseline's failures were in the commit window, which this change cannot reach.

**Left undone and reported, both diagnosed with reproductions during the investigation and both out of the scope that was chosen:** `revealCommit` is a **silent no-op for any commit newer than the last page load** — it pages forward only while `!eof`, so on exhausted history it notifies `evtReveal` for a sha the webview cannot find and does nothing (blame's `Show in Log`, `gittree.log.revealCommit`); on a >2000-commit repository it first burns up to 25 page reads walking *older* history for a sha that only exists at the top. It wants a `refresh()`-then-retry. And **inline blame keeps reading "Uncommitted changes" after the commit lands**: `BlameUi` subscribes to editor and document events but never to `repo.model.onDidChange`, `BlameService` caches on `(fsPath, docVersion)` and committing does not bump `docVersion`, and `BlameService.invalidate()` **has zero call sites in the tree** — proven with a harness test where `git blame` attributed the line to the new HEAD while `cursorCommit()` still returned the all-zero sha. Separately, `gittree.log.sort` is contributed in `package.json` but read nowhere.

POST-P14j ✅ 2026-08-30 — **Two silent-wrong-answer bugs behind the log: a reveal that reveals nothing, and blame that reports a sha history no longer has.** Both were found while investigating POST-P14i, both were reported rather than fixed there, and both were reproduced in the harness before anything was written.

**Bug A — `revealCommit` fired `evtReveal` whether or not it found the commit.** Six gestures funnel through it (blame's hover `Show in Log` and its status-bar item, `Go to Parent`, `Go to Child`, `Go to Hash/Branch/Tag`, the rebase editor's row action), and the webview answers a sha it cannot find with `if (idx !== -1)` and no else — so the miss was discarded at the far end of the RPC, where nothing could report it. **The dominant case is filter exclusion, not staleness**, which is what POST-P14i under-stated: `DEFAULT_FILTER` is `refs: 'HEAD'`, so any commit on an unmerged branch qualifies (reproduced: `rows 1 -> 1 selected=[]`), and under `Show File History`'s `paths` filter every parent that never touched the file does too (reproduced: `pathFilter rows=1 selected=[] parentInView=1be8f04`) — so in file-history mode `Go to Parent` was a dead button in the common case. `Go to Hash/Branch/Tag` was the worst of the six: it validates the ref with `rev-parse --verify`, *tells the user it is real*, and then showed nothing — and it returned `{ok: true}` while doing it. **Now:** the paging loop is `pageUntilLoaded`, `evtReveal` fires only on a hit, `revealCommit` returns whether the commit is on screen, and `goToRef`'s `ok` is that answer rather than the parse result. A miss under a narrowing filter is reported with **Show All Branches**, which widens to `{sort, refs: 'ALL'}` — only the sort survives, on the same rule the cross-repository move above it already followed — and retries once; a miss under an already-unfiltered view says the commit is not reachable from any branch or tag, which is exactly what a stale blame annotation hands over. The notification is deliberately not awaited: `Go to Parent` is a keypress and must not block on a toast nobody may click. `isUnfiltered` is a pure predicate in `logFilter.ts` beside `filterKey`, because that file's whole point is that every surface funnels through one filter shape; deciding "is this narrowing?" anywhere else would drift from `buildLogArgs`. **Left as a known cost:** a miss still walks up to 25 pages (50 000 rows at `PAGE_SIZE` 2000) before concluding, which on a repository that large is a multi-second stall in front of the message. The fix is a position probe over the pager's own argv, costed and ranked in the investigation, and deliberately not taken here.

**Bug B — blame kept the pre-commit answer, and handed the old sha to three actions.** Committing the lines you just edited left them reading `Uncommitted`; amending left them attributed to the previous commit. Reproduced: `reported=2d297fe truth=8b84ad7 docVersion=2 (cached at 2) reachableFromHEAD=false` — **the reported sha was reachable from nothing**, and the hover renders `Show in Log`, `Copy sha` and `Show Diff` from it, so `Copy sha` copied a sha the repository no longer has and `Show Diff` opened `gittree-rev:` URIs for it (rendering the old content, silently, while the object survives pre-gc). Four surfaces read the same cache: the gutter, the status bar, the hover, and `cursorCommit`, which `revealCursorCommit` then fed to Bug A. **Three independent causes, each sufficient:** `BlameUi` subscribed to four *editor* events and nothing to `repo.model.onDidChange`; `BlameService` keyed its cache on `docVersion` alone, and a commit leaves the buffer byte-for-byte identical so the version never moves — which is why even switching editors returned the stale answer through a cache *hit*; and `BlameService.invalidate()`, written and correct, **had zero call sites in the tree**. The trigger class is any history change that leaves the bytes alone — commit, `--amend`, `reset --soft`/`--mixed`, a rebase reproducing the same content — which is why it looked intermittent: anything that *does* change the file reloads the buffer and bumps the version. **Now:** the cache key is `(docVersion, headSha)` and `getCommitForLine` answers `undefined` rather than a line from a superseded HEAD — silence is recoverable, a wrong sha presented as fact is not — and a per-repository `model.onDidChange` watch, gated on the sha moving and coalesced at 300 ms, invalidates and repaints. The two halves do different jobs and neither suffices: the key makes a wrong answer impossible to *serve*, the watch makes the column repaint rather than wait for the next editor switch. **`headSha` is complete, not an approximation** — blame walks HEAD's ancestry and that is determined entirely by HEAD's sha, so no history change leaving HEAD alone can change the answer; content is already covered by `docVersion`, and staging is irrelevant (`git blame` reports an uncommitted line as all-zero whether staged or not). **`RepositoryModel.invalidationKey` is the §4 facility for exactly this and is deliberately not used:** it folds in `workdirGeneration`, which bumps on every worktree file event, so any save anywhere would evict every file's blame. Worth recording that the facility has **no users at all** — its only reader is `gittree.debug.showState` — and that both caches which needed it hand-rolled something weaker: `LogService` keys on `headSha` alone (the stale-chip bug POST-P14i fixed by invalidating), `BlameService` on `docVersion` alone (this one).

**A write-after-invalidate race, found by the suite and not by reading.** The first cut stamped the entry with the HEAD read *before* the git run, reasoning that a result straddling a move must not claim the newer sha. That is true and insufficient: reads are cancelled and requeued when a mutation lands, so a blame started before a commit finishes after the watcher's `invalidate()` has already run, and the entry is then written with a stamp that can never match again — every reader answers `undefined` **for good**, because nothing recomputes an entry that merely mismatches. A result that provably straddled a move is now returned to its caller and not cached at all; the watcher already has a re-blame scheduled, so it self-heals. **A second defect caught in review rather than by a test:** the coalescing timer was shared across repositories but the pending repository was not, so in a multi-root workspace a second HEAD move cancelled the first one's invalidation. The timer stays shared — only the active editor is annotated, so one re-blame serves all — and the pending set is now a map.

**A pre-existing harness leak, surfaced and fixed.** `phase02` T5 opens a multi-diff compare tab and never closed it. While one of those is the active tab, `vscode.window.showTextDocument` does **not** win `activeTextEditor` back — proven by polling it every 100 ms for 30 s and still reading `active=\main-only.txt`, phase02's `gittree-rev:` document, from a repository the P4 test was not even using. Everything `BlameUi` does resolves from the active editor, so the annotation was computed for someone else's file. This is not new and not caused by the product change (the same run showed `modelHead === gitHead`, so no stamp mismatch was in play); it had simply never been stepped on, and the new blame test walked straight into it. Fixed at the source — phase02 closes what it opens — and the blame test additionally starts from a known slate, because a shared window cannot promise one. Recorded here because the trap is general: **any suite that reads `vscode.window.activeTextEditor` is at the mercy of every tab left open before it.**

Testing: **+5 unit** (`isUnfiltered`: the DEFAULT filter is *not* unfiltered, sort never narrows, every narrowing field counts, and an empty `paths: []`/`authors: []` is not a filter because `buildLogArgs` gives git nothing for it either — asserted against `buildLogArgs` in the same test so the two cannot drift); **+2 integration** on `phase04` — a real commit on an unmerged branch reports rather than reveals under `refs: 'HEAD'` and then reveals normally under `refs: 'ALL'`, and an edit → commit → amend round trip where the annotation follows each step, asserted through a new `headRefreshCount` seam so the repaint is attributed to the head watcher rather than to something else that happened to touch the editor. The blame test polls rather than asserting once, deliberately: a blame that straddled a HEAD move is not cached *by design*. `waitFor` could not be used for it — its predicate is synchronous, so an async one resolves to a truthy Promise and passes without ever checking, which is worth knowing before someone reaches for it again.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **793 unit (+5) · 209 webview (unchanged, 3 skipped) · 262 integration passing + 2 pending, 2 failing**. **Neither failure is in this change.** P7-T2 is the 500 ms stash-latency flake (540 ms here) that has been an open owner decision since POST-P14f, and is the only failure common to every run. The other, P12-T5 `a two-path filter pages exactly git log -- a b`, was chased rather than written off because it sits in the log: it passes **23/23 with P12 in isolation**, and across four full runs of this change it failed twice and passed twice — one of those passes being a run that instead failed P1-T7 and both P5-T3s on `git checkout -f main` file locks. That is the signature of this machine, which is also running four unrelated vite dev servers: across every full run in this session the failing set was drawn from P1-T7, P3-T4 x2, P5-T3 x2, P7-T2, P12-T5, P13-T3 and Multi-root x2 — every one a `waitFor` or a file-lock, none in the blame or reveal path, and the *baseline* run on the unmodified tree failed P1-T7 and P7-T2 the same way. **One run was self-inflicted and is discarded:** the unit suite was started while a full integration run was still going, which produced two Multi-root failures and a 5 s timeout in a real-git StatusRefresher test that pass cleanly on their own — the harness note about running suites alone applies to more than the perf suite.

POST-P14k ✅ 2026-09-01 — **Worktrees moves to the tab IntelliJ keeps it in.** Raised from a side-by-side screenshot of the two sidebars: under GitTree's Commit window sit four collapsible sections — Branches, Worktrees, Local History, Stashes — and IntelliJ's Commit tool window has none of them. It is the Tier-1 failure again, *capability specified without container*: every one of the four is a real IntelliJ function, none of them lives where GitTree put it, and none was recorded in `docs/deviations.md`, so by the project's own rule they were gaps rather than accepted divergences. The placements were checked against JetBrains' own pages rather than assumed, and one of them corrected the plan: worktrees live in the **Git tool window** (*"press Alt+9, then click the down arrow and select Worktrees"*), and stashes are a **`Stash` tab in the Commit tool window** beside `Shelf` — not the sidebar rows this build grew, and not the "fold stashes into Shelf" shape first proposed here (that is IntelliJ's *optional* `Combine stashes and shelves in one tab`, off by default). This entry moves the first of the four. The Git tool window's strip becomes `Log | Console | Worktrees`; `WorktreesPane.tsx` renders one row per worktree with the branch as its label and the path beside it, and shows a `Locked` or `Prunable` badge **only** in those two states, which is JetBrains' own rule for that column. `WorktreesPaneBridge` builds the snapshot from the **existing** P13-T6 registry (`debugRegistry.get('worktrees')`) and dispatches every action back through the **existing** `gittree.worktree.*` commands, so the confirmation modal on Remove, the lock-reason prompt and the error reporting stay one implementation — the POST-P14d lesson about three surfaces of one registry drifting apart, applied before the drift could start. The pane holds `(repoRoot, path)` rather than live handles: both are re-resolved against a fresh `worktree list` on every dispatch, so a row clicked after the list moved underneath it is dropped instead of acting on whichever worktree now sits at that index. Two host details worth naming: the refresh ping cannot hang off `refsSub` the way the Branches pane's does, because that subscription follows the log's *single session repo* while this pane lists **every** repository — it gets its own per-repo watch set, rebuilt as repositories join and leave; and a repository that cannot be listed at all (mid-clone, permissions) contributes an empty group rather than taking the whole pane down.

Testing: **+9 unit** (`worktreesPaneBridge`: per-repo grouping in `core.repos` order, lock/prune reasons carried through, a rejecting repository degrading to an empty group, the empty state before P13-T6 has registered, the tree-node shape handed to the row commands, `add`/`prune` naming a repo and no row, a stale path dropped, an unknown repo dropped, and `refresh` running no git); **+5 webview** (the tab renders three fixture worktrees, the detached row names its HEAD, badges appear only when Locked/Prunable with the lock reason as the tooltip, Remove/Lock stay disabled on the main worktree and enable on a linked one, double-click posts `open`, New Worktree/Prune name a repository, and a host `worktreesChanged` event re-reads the list); **+1 integration** on `phase13` — a real `git worktree add` in repoC appears in the pane bridge's state under the right repository group with the main worktree still first, and a dispatch naming a path that never existed leaves the live worktree on disk.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **802 unit (+9) · 214 webview (+5, 3 skipped) · 265 integration passing + 1 pending, 1 failing**. The one failure is **not in this change**: P7-T2's 500 ms stash-latency bound (505 ms here), the open owner decision that has been the only common failure since POST-P14f. P12-T5 `a two-path filter pages exactly git log -- a b` failed once under full-suite load and passed both in isolation and on a clean re-run — the same load flake POST-P14j recorded. Ruled out as a cause of it: the new `worktreesChanged` event reaches a webview with no listener registered while the tab is unmounted, and `rpc.ts` drops unknown events silently rather than throwing.

POST-P14l ✅ 2026-09-01 — **Stashes move into the Commit tool window's `Stash` tab.** Second of the four sidebar sections (POST-P14k moved the first). The route chosen with the owner was "fold stashes into the Shelf tab", and checking JetBrains' own page before building corrected it: shelved changes live in the `Shelf` tab **and stashes in a `Stash` tab**, both in the Commit tool window, with *Combine stashes and shelves in one tab* (Settings | Version Control | Git | Stash) merging the two — **off by default**. So the strip is now `Commit | Shelf | Stash`, and `gittree.stash.combineWithShelf` (default `false`, as in IntelliJ) folds the third tab into the second. Building the faithful shape turned out cheaper than the approximation and closed a gap-analysis ❌ (§3.6 *Combine stashes and shelves*) on the way. `StashBridge` is `shelfBridge.ts`'s sibling in every respect — attached by the phase-07 register layer that owns the services, answering empty until it is, and dispatching every action back through the **existing** `gittree.stash.*` commands so the conflict routing on a pop and the confirmations stay one implementation. **One thing it deliberately does differently from the tree view it replaces: identity is the stash's sha, never `stash@{N}`.** The tree captured a live node at render time, so a row clicked after another stash was pushed or dropped acted on whichever stash had inherited that index — `drop` on the row labelled `stash@{1}` could delete a different stash. Every action from the tab carries `(repoRoot, sha)`, both re-resolved against a live `list()` on dispatch, and a sha that is no longer there drops the action instead of applying it somewhere else. Files are read on expand (`stashFiles`), because `git stash show` costs a git run per stash and the tab would otherwise pay for every stash to show one; the placeholder child exists so the row is expandable before its files are known, and the cache is dropped wholesale whenever the list moves rather than risk rendering a stale file list against a shifted row. Two smaller things worth naming: `createdAt` crosses the wire in **epoch ms**, matching `CommitShelfEntryDto` so both tabs render with the same helper — git reports unix seconds, and passing them straight through would have dated every stash to 1970; and flipping the combine setting on while the Stash tab is showing would have left the active tab naming one that no longer exists, which renders every panel `null`, so the window falls back to `Shelf`.

Testing: **+6 webview** (the third tab lists both fixture stashes; files are fetched on expand and **not** before, with the request keyed by sha; a toolbar action posts the sha and no index at all; row actions need a selection while `Stash Changes…` and `Clear` never do; a dropped stash leaves the list on the host's event; and `__mockCombineStashes` proves the tab count drops to two with the stash list rendered inside the Shelf tab); **+1 integration** on `phase07` — two real stashes in repoB, the older one asserted to have been pushed from `stash@{0}` to index 1, `filesFor` reading exactly its file, `createdAt` asserted to be milliseconds, then `drop` by the *older* stash's sha proving it removes that stash rather than the one now at its old index, and a repeat of the same dispatch proving a stale sha removes nothing. The P11-T3 handler-coverage test picks up the three new methods automatically (it walks `COMMIT_METHODS`).

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **802 unit (unchanged) · 220 webview (+6, 3 skipped) · 263 integration passing + 1 pending, 4 failing**. **None of the four is in this change**, and this machine is the reason there are four rather than the usual one: the full run took **21 minutes against a normal 8**, with VS Code reporting `Extension host is unresponsive` partway through, and four consecutive runs produced four *different* failure sets (1, 3, 2, then 4). Every one of them is a latency budget or an editor-timing race — P7-T2's 500 ms stash bound (979 ms), P0-T6's 500 ms watcher budget (541 ms), P12-T2's ref filter losing a race with an auto-refresh, and P13-T3's `git rev-parse HEAD` failing outright, alongside `unable to write symref for HEAD: Permission denied` in the log. The decisive check was re-running the StashService suite **alone**: setup clean, the new POST-P14l test passing, **5 of 6 passing, and the single failure P7-T2 at 720 ms** — the open owner decision that has been the only common failure since POST-P14f, now missing its bound by more than it used to because the box is busier. Nothing in this change touches the P0/P12/P13 paths; the one place it does touch P7 is a subscriber that fires `buildState()` — an in-memory `list()` map per repo, behind an early return while the commit view is unresolved. **Fixed in passing:** the new integration test first named its branch `p14l/stash-tab`, which the suite's teardown does not clean (it deletes `p7/*`); renamed to `p7/p14l-stash-tab` so the existing cleanup claims it.

POST-P14m ✅ 2026-09-01 — **The commit window stands alone in the sidebar again.** Last of the three-part answer to the screenshot that opened this: IntelliJ's Commit tool window is its tabs and nothing beneath them, and GitTree had grown four permanent collapsible sections under its own. POST-P14k moved worktrees to the Git tool window's `Worktrees` tab and POST-P14l gave stashes their `Stash` tab; this closes it by setting `gittree.branches`, `gittree.worktrees`, `gittree.localHistory` and `gittree.stashes` to `"visibility": "hidden"`. **Nothing is deleted and nothing becomes unreachable** — a hidden view is registered, so `<viewId>.focus` still reveals it and a user can tick any of the four back on from the container's ⋯ → Views menu. That is not a workaround, it is the mechanism `gittree.compare` has used since P13, and for Local History it is *closer* to IntelliJ than what it replaces: `gittree.history.show` already called `gittree.localHistory.focus`, so `Show Local History` now opens the view on demand exactly as IntelliJ's `Show History` opens its window, instead of the view standing open whether or not anyone asked for history. Branches needed nothing built at all — both of IntelliJ's branch surfaces already existed (the popup and the log's Branches pane), and the tree was a **third** copy of one registry, the arrangement POST-P14d caught offering four, one and nine menu rows for the same ref. After this the container holds one view that opens unconditionally, `gittree.commit`, and seven that are hidden or gated on a `when`. Recorded as **D26** rather than left implicit: keeping four opt-in panes IntelliJ has no equivalent of is a real divergence, its cost is one more surface per registry to keep honest, and the mitigation is structural — every relocated surface dispatches into the same `gittree.*` commands the trees do (`worktreesPaneBridge`, `stashBridge`, `shelfBridge`, `branchesPaneBridge`), so a divergence has to be introduced deliberately rather than by omission. A FAQ entry tells anyone who misses them where each surface went and how to bring the panes back.

Testing: **+1 integration** on `phase14` — the structural suite now asserts the shape rather than trusting it: each of the four is `visibility: hidden`, `gittree.commit` is not, and **no** view in the container opens unconditionally except the commit window (the last clause is the one that will catch the next pane added out of habit). The existing `EXPECTED_VIEWS` assertion is untouched and still passes: ids and manifest order did not change, only default visibility.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **802 unit (unchanged) · 220 webview (unchanged — no webview code in this task) · 264 integration passing + 1 pending, 4 failing**, `phase14` 16/16 including the new assertion. **None of the four failures is in this change**, and each was checked rather than assumed: P1-T7 `commit from the commit window` timed out at 30 s under load but passes **in isolation in 2.2 s** (its own comments record two earlier hardenings against full-suite load); P7-T2 is the 500 ms stash bound again (566 ms), the open owner decision; P12-T5 `a two-path filter` is the flake POST-P14j already recorded; and P14 branch-menu `the tracked bit appears and disappears with the upstream` is a 20 s `waitFor` on a refresh. This task changes four `visibility` fields in the manifest and adds a test — it touches no runtime code path at all, which is the strongest evidence available that the failures are the machine. Standing caveat for this session's three entries: **no clean full run was achieved on this box** (five runs gave 1, 3, 2, 4 and 4 failures, never the same set, with wall time drifting 8 → 21 minutes and one run reporting `Extension host is unresponsive`), so confidence rests on isolated re-runs plus targeted tests rather than on a green suite.

POST-P14n ✅ 2026-09-01 — **The sidebar panes are gated on a setting, because POST-P14m's hiding never reached anyone.** The owner installed the POST-P14m build and the four sections were still there. The manifest was right — the packaged VSIX carried `"visibility": "hidden"` on all four — and the build was right, since the same screenshot showed `Commit | Shelf | Stash` and `Log | Console | Worktrees` working. **`visibility` is only a first-registration default.** VS Code persists a view container's layout the first time it renders and never consults the default again, so the change reached new profiles only — and a fresh profile is exactly what the integration window is, which is why `phase14` stayed green while every real user kept seeing the panes. A test that asserts a manifest field cannot see that difference; this is the same "shape, not capability" trap the Tier-1 review named, one level down — the *manifest* was the shape, and the shape a user sees is what VS Code does with it. The fix is a `when` clause, which is evaluated live and removes the view outright: all four are gated on `config.gittree.sidebar.extraViews` (default `false`, contributed under the general settings section), so they disappear on upgrade regardless of saved layout, and turning the setting on brings them back as collapsed sections with no reload. **The gate's one real hazard is that a view which is not contributed cannot be focused**, which would have made `Show Local History` and the branches popup's last header button silent no-ops. Both now arm their own context key first — `gittree.localHistory.active` and `gittree.branches.active`, the second clause in those two `when`s — which is the pattern `gittree.compare` has used since P13. For Local History that lands *closer* to IntelliJ than the pane it replaces: `Show History` opens the window when asked, rather than standing open. Verified before writing anything: `createTreeView` on a view whose `when` is false is safe, because `CompareService.register` has been doing exactly that at activation since P13.

Testing: **+3 integration** on `phase14`, replacing the POST-P14m manifest assertion. The load-bearing one is `the gate overrides a layout that already has the pane showing`: it turns the setting on, focuses the stashes pane and waits for `TreeView.visible` to be genuinely true — persisting that layout — then turns the setting off and requires the pane to go away. That is the upgrade case in miniature and it is the check that would have caught the first attempt; a manifest assertion passes either way. A second test proves `Show Local History` still reveals its view with the setting off (the no-op hazard), and restores the context key afterwards because later suites share the window. The third pins the manifest: gated on the setting, *not* also `hidden` (which would mean enabling the setting showed nothing), with the two on-demand clauses present. `phase07` and `phase09` expose their `TreeView` so the gate can be asserted against a live view rather than a string. **Also fixed, and it was genuinely this change's doing:** `multiRoot`'s `one gesture commits every repository` began failing consistently. The commit itself was correct — both HEADs moved with the typed message — but the closing assertion read the commit window's `targets` immediately after a `waitFor` that polls **git** for HEAD, and the window's state is one refresh behind that. It was relying on winning a race it had no reason to win, and rendering fewer views at startup shifted the timing enough to lose it. Synchronized on the model observing the commit, the way `phase01`'s T7 already does for the same gap.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **267 integration passing + 2 pending, 2 failing** in a normal 8-minute run, `phase14` 18/18. The two failures are the standing pair and neither is in this change: P5-T3 died on `git checkout -f main` → `unable to write symref for HEAD: Permission denied`, a Windows lock on the fixture, and P12-T5 is the flake POST-P14j recorded. **P7-T2 passed this run** (it had been failing all session at 505–979 ms), which is the clearest sign yet that the session's earlier noise was the machine: unit budgets were missing by 2× at the same time, and stashing this work and re-running proved the same unit tests failed on the parent commit with none of it present. Every suspect failure in the 26-failure run before this one was chased to ground rather than assumed — P2 clean alone, P4's `commit actions` failing **identically on the parent** (`HEAD~1` does not exist when that suite runs alone, so it is not self-contained), and a stale `gittree.commit.window: "native"` in the test profile ruled out as inert because P14-T2 retired the key and nothing reads it any more.

POST-P14o ✅ 2026-09-06 — **Fetch is a project operation, as IntelliJ's is.** The owner asked how to fetch every repository in a multi-root workspace and the honest answer was "you cannot" — `gittree.fetch` inferred **one** root from the active editor (`pickRepo`: active editor's repo, else `core.repos[0]`) and reported nothing about which one it picked, so in a five-root workspace the palette entry was a coin flip whose result was invisible. IntelliJ has no such gesture: the ⤡ button in the branches-popup header fetches every root, and there is no per-root fetch in its UI at all, because fetch only moves `refs/remotes` and `FETCH_HEAD` and scoping it buys nothing. The one place GitTree already agreed was auto-fetch, which loops `core.repos`; the manual path was the one that did not. **The scope is now decided by argument presence**: `gittree.fetch` with an explicit `repoRoot` still fetches exactly that repository — so the force-with-lease retry prompt and any keybinding that names a root keep their narrow behaviour — and with no argument it fans out across every root. A single-root workspace sees no change whatsoever, which is most workspaces. `gittree.fetch.all` is the same fan-out under a bindable id, and the branches-popup header button now calls the no-argument form, with its tooltip reading *Fetch all repositories* whenever there is more than one root.

Three things the fan-out has to get right, and each is where the work went. **Roots with no remote are skipped, not failed** — `git remote` is asked per root, because remote *refs* are absent until the first fetch, which is exactly when a fetch is most wanted; a local-only root can only answer "No remote repository specified", and in a mixed workspace that is noise. **One failure never hides the rest**: `runFetchAll` records the outcome and keeps going, and the summary names up to three failures in one notification instead of firing `presentGitError` per root. The cost is real and deliberate — a fan-out failure loses the actionable per-error dialog, which the single-root path still gets. **The pool is bounded at 4**, matching `OPEN_CONCURRENCY`, and Cancel stops scheduling so unstarted roots come back `cancelled` and the summary can say so.

The seam that made this safe is `Operation.quietProgress`. Every fetch is a `mutate`, and `vscodeProgressAdapter` puts each one in the status bar and **escalates it to a cancellable notification after 2 s** — seven roots on a slow network would have stacked seven of them. `quietProgress` skips the adapter and nothing else: same lane, same serialization, same `onDidFinishMutation`, same failure propagation. It also fixes a papercut nobody had filed: **auto-fetch was never silent**, so a background fetch every 180 s raised progress UI and, past two seconds, a notification, where IntelliJ's background fetch says nothing at all. `anyToken` joins the fan-out's Cancel with the queue's own token, so cancelling actually reaches the running `git fetch` rather than only stopping the queue. **Fetch also reached the VCS Operations Popup for the first time** — that popup enumerates `dialogRegistry` and fetch has no dialog, so it had simply never been there; it dispatches `gittree.fetch.all` with an explicit empty argument list, because the popup otherwise passes the active root and would have scoped it straight back down.

Testing: **+21 unit** — `fetchAll.test.ts` (input-ordered results regardless of completion order; one failing root does not stop the others and only the first line of the git error is carried; never rejects even when every root fails; the pool peaks at exactly `concurrency`; cancelling leaves unstarted roots `cancelled`; progress counts up; an empty workspace spawns no worker), `cancellation.test.ts` for `anyToken` (either input wins, a pre-cancelled input counts, listeners fire once, dispose really detaches), two on `OperationQueue` (`quietProgress` skips the adapter while still serializing FIFO and still firing `onDidFinishMutation`; failures still reach the caller) and four on the ops model. **+5 integration** in a new `syncFetchAll` suite on repoD/repoE, each wired to a local bare remote in the temp dir — no network, no accounts: no argument fetches **both** roots, an explicit root fetches **only** that one (asserted against the other's unchanged tracking sha, which is the regression that matters), `gittree.fetch.all` behaves identically, remote-less roots keep their `lastFetchAt` untouched, and every root the fan-out touched gets a fresh one. Both roots are unwired in teardown so the suites after it find repoD/repoE as they left them.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **823 unit (+21) · 220 webview (unchanged — no webview code in this task) · 266 integration passing + 1 pending, 9 failing**, the new `syncFetchAll` suite 5/5 **inside the full run**. **None of the nine is in this change, and the two worth suspecting were proven rather than assumed.** `multiRoot`'s `an explicit repository argument stays scoped to it` and `amend rewrites only its own repository` are the only failures whose subject is even adjacent to a fan-out, and they fail **identically on the parent commit with this work stashed** — 6 passing, 2 failing, same assertion (`repoE keeps its ticks`), in a 45-second isolated run with nothing else on the box. They are the commit window's state being one refresh behind the git poll the assertion waits on, the same race POST-P14n fixed once for the sibling test. The rest: P7-T2's 500 ms stash bound (1574 ms) and P0-T6's 500 ms watcher budget (1176 ms), both standing; P13-T3's added-file pathspec race; and two P5 rebase tests which, re-run alone, **failed a different pair** (`revert of a merge commit`, `rewrite on a published branch`) — and that second one passes on its own in 27 s. The run took **37 minutes against a normal 8** with `Extension host is unresponsive` throughout, which is the same degraded box the last three entries recorded. Unit-side, the same check was run: `npm run check` reported 25 failures, the parent commit with everything stashed reported **32**, every one of them a real-git spawn timing out against a 5 s default (`reports in-progress merge state` passes at **4999 ms**), and serializing file execution (`--no-file-parallelism`) drops it to 4 — all pre-existing, none touching a fetch path.

POST-P14p ✅ 2026-09-06 — **The branches popup stops guessing which repository it is about.** POST-P14o fixed the fetch half of the owner's question; this is the other half, and it is the one that was actually a design fault rather than a missing command. GitTree's popup was bound to a single `RepoHandle` chosen by `pickRepo` — the active editor's root, else `core.repos[0]` — and **never named it**, so in the owner's seven-repository workspace every branch action landed in a repository the popup would not tell you about. IntelliJ never does this: in a multi-root project the popup *is* the project — a row per repository showing its current branch, then `Common Local Branches` / `Common Remote Branches` / `Common Tags` — and scope is picked, not inferred. That is now the shape. **With more than one root** the popup opens at the project level; picking a repository row drills into that root's own popup, which is the popup that already existed, unchanged, now titled with the repository's name and carrying `‹ Back` up to the project (`forceBack`, since at prefix `''` there is a level above for the first time). **With one root nothing changes at all** — same entry point, same items, same title, same buttons. The status-bar widget still names its own repository, and now opens the project level with that repository's row pre-selected, so the widget's meaning survives: you land on your repository, inside the whole project rather than alone in it.

**The level decides the scope, and that is the whole rule.** Inside a repository the hub verbs carry its root exactly as they always did. At the project level they carry no argument, which for `Commit…` means every root with something ticked (`runCommitAcross`, which has answered the no-argument form since P11) and for `Fetch` means every root (POST-P14o). It is a real behaviour change for a multi-root user — the popup's `Commit…` used to be scoped to the guessed repository — and it is the change that makes the verb mean what its position says it means. `Update Project…` and `Push…` still open their single-repository dialogs, because those dialogs are built per root; that limit is recorded in **D27** rather than papered over. `New branch` leaves the header at the project level for the same reason its common-row cousins do: it asks for a name *inside* the per-repository call, so fanning it out would ask seven times.

**Common rows are where the all-roots operations live** — the gap analysis §3.10 row *"Multi-repo, Execute branch operations on all roots"*, open since the Tier-1 review. A ref qualifies as common only if **every** root carries it; anything else has no coherent all-roots operation to offer. Accepting one checks it out everywhere; its `›` submenu offers `Update`, `Merge into the current branch`, `Rebase the current branch onto it`, `Pull into the current branch using Merge`/`… Rebase` and `Push`. Nothing here is a second implementation: each is a `branchActions.ts` entry dispatched by id once per root, so the popup, the tree and the log's Branches pane still cannot drift. Only the **label** is rewritten, because the registry's labels interpolate the current branch (`Merge 'x' into 'main'`) and at the project level the roots need not share one — a label naming the first root's branch would be a lie about the other six.

Three judgement calls inside that, each of which changes how the feature feels. **Offer an action that applies in only some roots, and say how many** — `Checkout in 5 of 7 repositories`. The first cut required every root, and reviewing it caught that this hides `Checkout` the moment one root is already on the branch, which is the ordinary state of a project tracking one branch and precisely when you want to bring the stragglers across. **Skip the roots where it does not apply rather than attempt them**, and when it applies nowhere say "nothing to do" instead of looking like a dead click. **Stop at the first refusal**, naming the roots already done — the idiom `runCommitAcross` set, and the alternative is starting a merge in roots 3…7 while root 2 sits in conflict. `runCommonAction` takes the roots as a parameter rather than reading `core.repos`, which is both the honest contract and what lets the test drive it over two repositories instead of every repository in the shared window.

Testing: **+17 integration** in a new `projectPopup` suite. The pure half, against fabricated input: section order (verbs → repositories → common refs) and the verb list matching the repository level's; every repository listed, named and carrying its branch; the three common sections carrying their *kind*, since that is what gates the verbs; typing hiding the verbs while repositories and common rows stay searchable; a detached root saying `detached at abcdef1` rather than naming a branch it does not have; `commonRefNames` meaning present-in-every-root (and yielding nothing when one root has none of that kind, the case where "common to all" of an empty set would otherwise mean "everything the others have"); the partial label (`Checkout in 1 of 2 repositories`); the kind gate (pull is a remote-row verb, update a local one) and an assertion that **no prompting action is ever offered on a common row** — `newFrom`, `rename`, `delete`, `deleteOnRemote`, `newWorktree` by name, which is the D27 boundary made executable; that every `COMMON_ACTIONS` id is a real registry action, so a renamed action cannot leave a dead row; and `forceBack`. The live half runs `runCommonAction` over repoD and repoE for real: a branch created in both, checked out in both by one call, then **the same call again as a no-op** because it now applies in neither, then both roots handed back on their original branch with the branch deleted. Two more use a probe action to pin the fan-out rules directly — a root the action does not apply to is never entered, and a root that refuses stops the one after it.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **823 unit (unchanged — the popup imports vscode, so its tests are integration, as P13-T5's are) · 220 webview (unchanged — no webview code) · 242 integration passing + 2 pending, 26 failing**, the new `projectPopup` suite **17/17 inside the full run** and `syncFetchAll` still 5/5 beside it. **The 26 is the box, not the change, and it is worth recording what the box did**: `git init -q` failed with `Mingw-w64 runtime failure: VirtualProtect failed with code 0x5af` — Windows refusing to let git.exe start at all — and 24 of the 26 are that same class, `execFileSync` git calls in test scaffolding dying outright and taking their `before all` hooks (and the suites behind them) with them. **Only two of the twenty-six are AssertionErrors**, and they are the identical `multiRoot` pair POST-P14o already proved fails on the parent commit with all this work stashed. Every branch-adjacent suite was then re-run rather than argued about: `P13 branches hub` + `P2 branches` + `P14 branch menu` — the three that consume the popup and the registry it dispatches into — **30/30 green** in a targeted run, including the `P14` upstream test that had timed out in the full run; and `P12 git tool window`, which failed **five** tests in the full run, failed a completely **different two** on re-run (`a two-path filter`, the flake POST-P14j recorded, and `a fixup of a non-ancestor`), both of which then **passed in isolation in 14 seconds**. A different failure set on every run over the same code is the signature this box has shown all session; the standing caveat from the POST-P14m/n entries therefore still holds — **no clean full run has been achieved here**, and confidence rests on targeted re-runs plus the parent-commit comparison rather than on a green suite.

POST-P14q ✅ 2026-09-10 — **The status-bar widget survives a repository leaving the workspace.** Reported as *"sometimes, after taking some actions on the Branch popup, the GitTree branch button disappears"*, and it is not a popup bug at all — the popup is only what reliably shakes it loose. A bar is visible when `activeRepoKey() === key`, and `activeRepoKey` reads **three** inputs: the active editor, `repoForUri` resolved against the live handle set, and `core.repos[0]` as the fallback. Only the first had an invalidation trigger. `registerPhase01` subscribed `onDidAddRepo` — and `attach` early-returns for a repository it already knows, so a join never re-evaluated anybody else — and subscribed `onDidRemoveRepo` **not at all**, alone among the consumers of that event (`branchesView`, `commitWindowView`, `blameUi` and `logView` all take it). So when a repository left, its own items were disposed with it and every surviving bar kept the `visible = false` it had last computed. Nothing recomputed them. **The status bar went empty and stayed empty**, until an unrelated event — switching editors, or a git operation in whichever repository had inherited `repos[0]` — happened to call `update()`.

**Why the Branch popup is where it shows up.** Its actions write into `.git`; `createWorkspaceWatcher` turns a `.git` *entry* event into `pokeRescan`; `rescan` re-probes every candidate with `rev-parse --show-toplevel`; and `probe` folds **any** failure into `root: undefined` while `getRepoRoot` returns `undefined` on any non-zero exit — so a repository that transiently fails to probe is silently absent from `roots` and is removed. Seven roots and a project-level action (Fetch all, Update Project, a common-ref fan-out) is a burst of concurrent git spawns over repositories being written to, which is exactly when one `rev-parse` loses on Windows. Hence *sometimes*. Nor does it heal itself: `handles.set` appends, so a repository that comes back lands at the **tail** and `core.repos[0]` has permanently changed identity.

The fix is the one sentence the wiring was missing — membership is an input to visibility, so membership events re-evaluate all of it. One `updateAll()`, called after `attach` on add, and on remove, which `Core` fires once the handle is out of the map and its own bar disposed, so it sees exactly the set that remains; the editor listener now calls the same function instead of inlining the loop. **Deliberately not changed**: `probe` swallowing a transient failure (hardening it would reduce the churn but not close the hole — worktree creation and workspace-folder edits remove handles legitimately), and `attach`'s `statusBars.has(key)` guard, which would strand a repository with no controller at all if a re-add ever preceded the old handle's disposal — unreachable today because `rescan` removes before it adds, and now written down as load-bearing rather than left as an accident.

Testing: **+1 integration** in a new `statusBarVisibility` suite, plus the `StatusBarController.visible` seam it needs — `StatusBarItem` exposes no `visible`, and `titles` cannot see this regression because a hidden item keeps its text. The sixth repository is built **inside repoA** rather than added as a sixth workspace folder, for the reason `discovery.test.ts` already records (a workspace folder perturbs every other suite in this window); repoA is the safe host because its own suite runs earlier and nothing after this file asserts on it, and `.git/info/exclude` keeps it out of repoA's status while it exists. Three steps: a repository joining must not add a second visible widget; focusing a file inside it hands it the widget, since `repoForUri` takes the longest matching root; then it leaves **while its file stays open and focused**, so no editor event fires to paper over the missing invalidation — which is the reported failure exactly. It leaves via the ignore setting rather than the filesystem: `Core.rescan` takes the identical removal path either way, and on Windows the repository's own `GitDirWatcher` holds `.git` open, so deleting it there fails with EPERM for reasons that have nothing to do with the subject (the first cut did exactly that and died on it). **The test was proven to catch the bug rather than assumed to** — with only the `onDidRemoveRepo` handler stubbed out it fails on its own assertion message, *the widget vanished when the repository it was showing left the workspace*, and passes with it restored.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **823 unit (unchanged — the defect is a vscode subscription, so its test is integration) · 220 webview + 3 skipped (unchanged — no webview code in this task, re-run anyway) · 290 integration passing + 1 pending, 1 failing**, the new suite green inside the full run. The one failure is **P7-T2's 500 ms stash bound at 527 ms**, the standing flake this log has carried since P7 and on which the owner still owes a decision. `P12-T5 a two-path filter` failed in two earlier full runs and passed in this one and in isolation — the same flake POST-P14p recorded, in a suite that runs *before* this one and does no repository churn. This is the cleanest full run recorded here in several entries; the box behaved for once.

POST-P14r ✅ 2026-09-15 — **The status-bar widget stops being a second branch readout.** The reported defect was "after clicking Fetch in the branch popup, the GitTree branch button disappears", and POST-P14q's diagnostic answered it in one dump: seven repositories present, seven controllers registered, the active one `visible: true` with correct text — and invisible. GitTree was showing the item; VS Code was not drawing it. The cause is width. `renderStatusBar` printed `$(arrow-down) ${behind} $(arrow-up) ${ahead}` with nothing bounding either side, so a fetch against a repository 26 050 commits behind turned a one-glyph `$(sync)` into ten characters of digits. The status bar is a fixed strip, GitTree's entries sit at the low-priority end of the left group, and the pair went over the edge. **The flicker the reporter also saw is the same fact from the other side:** mid-fetch the item is `$(sync~spin)` — narrow, so it comes back — and the counts return the moment the fetch lands.

**The owner then named the real problem, which is not width.** VS Code's built-in Git extension already contributes a branch name *and* ahead/behind counts, so GitTree's pair was a duplicate — four status-bar items saying the same thing — and no amount of narrowing fixes a duplicate. The resolution is a distinction the two references make and we had missed: **VS Code's widget is a status readout that happens to be clickable; IntelliJ's is a menu opener that happens to show the branch.** GitTree's value there was never the readout — it is the branches popup. So the widget is now an *action*: `$(git-branch)$(chevron-down)`, one constant width, opening the popup, with branch, upstream and exact counts moved into its tooltip. It grows for exactly two things, both of which the built-in widget does not show — an in-progress operation (`$(warning) REBASING 2/5`) and a conflict count. Recorded as **D28**, and the index table gained the D26/D27 rows it had been missing since those deviations were written.

**The readout is conditional, not deleted.** `gittree.statusBar` is `auto` | `button` | `full` | `off`, defaulting to `auto`: the compact button while something else is drawing a branch readout, the `full` widget — branch plus counts, capped at `99+` the way IntelliJ caps its own — when the built-in Git extension is absent or `git.enabled` is false. Detection is deliberately conservative and says so: VS Code exposes no way to see that a user hid those entries from the status bar's own right-click menu, so the explicit modes are the way out and `auto` never guesses past what it can observe. GitTree only ever *reads* `git.enabled` (the existing `unsetGitEnabled` guarantee is untouched). The standalone sync item is gone — `Update Project… Ctrl+T` and `Push… Ctrl+Shift+K` are rows 1 and 3 of the popup, which is where IntelliJ puts them — and the rebase row survives as its own item with its own id, because "continue / skip / abort" is a second verb and inheriting slot 1 from the deleted sync item would have hidden it for anyone who had hidden that one. **`gittree.branches.open` finally has a keybinding** (`Ctrl+Alt+G C`): the widget was its only discoverable trigger, which is a poor position for the surface the whole extension is built around.

The shaping rules moved to `ui/statusBarModel.ts`, vscode-free, because width is what broke and width is now asserted rather than assumed. Testing: **+18 unit** on the model — `auto` resolving both ways and explicit modes never second-guessed; `99+` capping; the button being byte-identical across clean, 26 050-behind and detached snapshots; a fetch not changing its glyph count; the growth rule firing for operations and conflicts and for *nothing else* (a dirty, diverged, detached, upstream-gone snapshot still renders exactly `$(git-branch)$(chevron-down)`); zero sides omitted; `off` contributing nothing even mid-rebase. **+1 integration** driving the setting end to end — `auto` → button, `full` → readout, `off` → not merely hidden — with the middle step proving the configuration listener is wired, since nothing but the setting changes between the two reads. P1's T5 now asserts the divergence readout in the mode that owns it and the tooltip in the mode that does not, and `titles` clears the text of a retired item so the debug dump can never report a label that is off screen.

Gates: typecheck+lint clean, **841 unit (+18) · 220 webview + 3 skipped (unchanged — no webview code) · 291 integration passing + 1 pending, 2 failing**, and `docs/settings.md` regenerated (its generator test is what caught the new setting). Both failures are the standing pair this log has carried for several entries and neither is in this change: **P7-T2's 500 ms stash bound at 566 ms**, still awaiting an owner decision, and **P12-T5 `a two-path filter`**, the flake POST-P14p recorded — it failed here, passed in isolation, and lives in a suite that runs before this one and touches no status bar. `P1 core SCM` T7 failed once mid-session and passed on re-run: the commit-window race the harness notes already warn about, not a regression from this work.

POST-P14s ✅ 2026-09-15 — **The status-bar widget had one entry and seven owners.** Two previous entries claimed this defect and neither fixed it; the owner reported it still happening after POST-P14r, which is what forced the re-derivation. POST-P14q blamed a missing `onDidRemoveRepo` invalidation (a real bug, unrelated). POST-P14r blamed width — an unbounded `$(arrow-down) 26050 $(arrow-up) 10893` falling off the end of a crowded status bar — and its own prediction refuted it: a three-glyph button disappeared exactly as the wide widget had.

**The actual cause is `vscode.window.createStatusBarItem(id, …)`, whose `id` the API documents as "must be unique within the extension".** It is not a label. The extension host derives the entry's routing key from it, where the id-less overload hands out a fresh key per item — VS Code 1.129's bundle has it plainly: `_entryId = i4(extensionIdentifier, id)` for the id overload against `_entryId = String(ID_GEN++)` for the other. GitTree created one `StatusBarController` per repository, and each created its own item with the *same* id, `gittree.status.0`. Seven repositories did not give us seven entries. They gave us **one entry with seven owners**, and the two halves of the API are asymmetric: `show()` defers through a `setTimeout(…, 0)` to `setOrUpdateEntry` (last writer wins the text), while `hide()` runs immediately and calls `$disposeEntry`, which **removes the shared entry from the status bar** — the one the active repository had put there.

**That asymmetry is the whole "sometimes".** A full pass over every controller ends correctly, because the hides land synchronously and the active repository's deferred show then recreates the entry — so the widget is usually present, and every test that drove a full pass passed. But any *single* inactive controller updating on its own runs `hide()` with no show behind it. `fetchOne` brackets each repository with `setFetching(true/false)`, so a project-wide fetch walks every controller in turn: the active repository's own fetch flashes the button back, a later repository's `setFetching(false)` takes it away, and it stays away. The reporter's "I might see it appear again for a while and then disappear again" is that sequence, exactly. A background status refresh in any other repository does the same thing with no fetch involved, which is the plain "sometimes".

**Why it survived two rounds of investigation, including a debug dump.** The destruction happens on the main thread under a key no controller can see. From inside the extension everything was right and said so: `visible` was `true`, `item.text` was correct, `statusBarDiagnostics()` agreed — and the widget was not on screen. POST-P14r read that dump as "we show it, VS Code does not draw it", which was true, and then attributed it to crowding, which was not. `StatusBarItem` exposes no `visible` getter, so no assertion over extension-side state can ever catch this class of bug.

**The fix removes the topology, not a symptom.** `StatusBarHost` owns the items — one per *slot*, for the whole extension — and renders whichever repository is active; `StatusBarController` keeps per-repository state and answers what it *would* contribute, touching no `StatusBarItem` at all. One owner per id is then structural rather than a rule to remember. Everything funnels through a single `render()` in `registerPhase01`, so there is exactly one answer to "what is on the status bar". The ids are unchanged, which is what keeps a user's "hidden" choice attached to the right row. POST-P14r's *shape* is untouched and still argued from duplication with VS Code's own readout — D28 has been corrected to say so, since it had claimed the width story as its motivation.

Testing: the discriminating assertion is the API contract itself, on the ids GitTree actually holds — **+1 integration** asserting no id appears twice and that the item count tracks slots, not repositories. **It was proven to catch the defect**: reinstating the per-repository topology under the new diagnostics fails it with `gittree.status.0, gittree.status.0, gittree.status.0, gittree.status.0, gittree.status.0` across the window's five repositories, and passes on restoring the fix. **+1 integration** for the behaviour that failure produced — every inactive repository doing exactly what the fetch fan-out does to it, with the widget required not to move; it is a guard, not a discriminator, and it passed against the broken topology too. Diagnostics gained `rendered` and `itemIds` so the next report of this shape is answerable from the dump rather than from a bundle read.

Gates (scoped to the change, per the owner's standing instruction to stop running the full suite): typecheck + lint clean on the changed files; **38 integration passing, 0 failing** across `P1 core SCM`, `P0-T7 discovery`, `P14 owning the SCM surface` and `status bar visibility` — the four suites that read the status-bar seams or the debug dump. No unit test imports `statusBar.ts` (`vitest related` finds none); the vscode-free `statusBarModel.ts` and its 18 tests are untouched by this change.

POST-P14t ✅ 2026-09-17 — **The status-bar button says whose it is.** The owner's screenshot made the problem plain: GitTree's `⑂ ∨` sat two entries from the built-in Git extension's `⑂ develop`, drawn with **the same codicon**. POST-P14r had reshaped the widget into an action to stop duplicating VS Code's readout, and then drew that action with VS Code's own branch glyph and no label — so it still read as a fourth git entry rather than as GitTree, and someone who had just installed the extension had nothing to aim at. Compactness had been taken as a licence to say nothing.

**Three arguments were offered for a wordmark and they are not equally strong.** *Discoverability* is the real one: the walkthrough exists but is the first thing anyone dismisses, and the status bar is where a new user actually looks. *Distinguishability* is real too, and was partly self-inflicted — we chose `$(git-branch)`. *Branding in screenshots* is a bonus and was not allowed to drive the decision. The owner's own stated worry — that a text label would lose the smooth icon→spinner transition on fetch — turned out to be the opposite: the mark and the label are separate glyphs, so the spinner swaps only the mark while the label and chevron hold their position. The old button had nothing but the chevron anchoring that swap; the new one cannot reflow at all.

**What ships:** `$(list-tree) GitTree $(chevron-down)` in `button` mode, and the `gittree-panel` container's icon moves off `$(git-branch)` to match — the same duplication, in a place nobody had looked. `full` mode is deliberately untouched and keeps `$(git-branch)` / `$(git-commit)`: there the widget *is* the branch readout, the branch name is its label, and nothing else is drawing one. The rule is worth stating because it is not obvious: **the name appears where the widget would otherwise have no words.** `$(list-tree)` is a placeholder chosen for what it is *not* — `$(git-branch)`, `$(git-commit)` and `$(source-control)` are all already in this strip. The owner has since produced a real mark (`media/gittree-mark.svg`, a "T" built from commit nodes), and it belongs here as soon as `contributes.icons` carries it as a font glyph, which is the only route an SVG has into status-bar text.

**Two stale claims corrected while in the files.** `ui/statusBarModel.ts` and its test both opened by asserting that width was why the branch button disappeared. POST-P14s disproved that — a shared `StatusBarItem` id was the cause — and the comments had outlived the belief. The caps are still right and still tested; only the diagnosis was wrong. D28 gained the same correction plus the reasoning above.

Testing: **+1 unit** asserting the property that regressed into existence — the button names the product and its mark is *not* `$(git-branch)` or `$(source-control)`, and still ends in the chevron that claims it opens a menu; the fetch test additionally pins that only the mark swaps. Existing literals updated across 1 unit and 2 integration suites. **19 unit** on the model.

Gates (scoped, per the owner's standing instruction): typecheck + lint clean, **19 unit**, **28 integration passing / 0 failing** across `P1 core SCM`, `P14 owning the SCM surface` and `status bar visibility`, and `docs/settings.md` regenerated. **A flake worth recording:** `P1 core SCM` T1 failed in four of seven runs with `git checkout -b p1-side` → "a branch named 'p1-side' already exists", on a fixture `mkdtemp`-ed fresh for each run, where only that test ever creates or deletes that branch. It is not this change — the diff to that file is one line in T5, the suite passes unmodified, and it also passes *with* the change — but **the mechanism is not understood**: no lock error precedes it in any captured log, and `gitWithRetry` rethrows non-lock failures without retrying, so the "already exists" is reported by the first attempt. It is load-dependent (more frequent with more suites in the grep). Worth its own investigation rather than an assumed cause.

POST-P14u ✅ 2026-09-17 — **Two sidebar sections: Repositories, and Branches that follows it.** Raised by the owner from two directions at once. *Discoverability:* a VS Code user who has just installed GitTree does not know to click a status-bar button to reach branches, and wants a surface that stays open rather than a popup that can be dismissed mid-thought. *Portability:* **Cursor has no status bar**, confirmed from the owner's own window — the Git tool window and the activity-bar icon are both there and the bottom strip simply is not, so every pixel of POST-P14r–t is invisible to a Cursor user. The conclusion is stronger than the request: **no primary entry point may depend on the status bar existing.**

**The framing was half wrong, and worth correcting before building.** The owner opened by saying this goes against the project's IntelliJ-fidelity principle. A persistent branches surface *is* IntelliJ's — P12-T2 built it, in the Git tool window where JetBrains keeps it. What was missing was not the capability but the container a VS Code user opens, which is POST-P14k's *capability specified without container* pointed the other way: the placement was faithful and nobody found it. The genuinely new things are the Repositories section (IntelliJ has no repositories window) and exclusive focus (IntelliJ has no current-repository notion at all — D27 is the opposite stance). Recorded as **D29**, which also amends **D26**: Branches is no longer one of its four opt-in panes.

**Three amendments to the owner's spec, all agreed before building.** (1) The Branches view is the section the owner disliked, *rebuilt* — not a fourth surface beside it. It keeps the id `gittree.branches`, so a user's persisted layout and all 21 `view/item/context` clauses carry over; the all-repositories tree kept its provider and moved to `gittree.branchesLegacy` behind the existing `gittree.sidebar.extraViews`, because `phase02` and `phase14branchmenu` assert against that provider directly and because the owner asked for it to stay restorable. (2) **No repository dropdown in Branches.** The spec had one directly beneath a list of the same repositories, with two-way binding between them — the same duplication the owner had just objected to in the status bar, plus two selectors to keep in step. The section names its repository in its header instead; one selector cannot desync from itself. (3) **Focus is a view filter, not a mode** — see below.

**The design fault this avoids.** "Which repository?" already had three answers: `pickRepo`'s active-editor inference (copied into six files, ~20 call sites), the log's own root selector, and the status bar's `activeRepoKey`. A fourth that drove *some* commands would let the sidebar name one repository while `Ctrl+K` committed another, with nothing on screen to say so. So focus scopes the two sections that draw it and nothing else, which is what **both** references do — VS Code's Source Control selection filters that view while its status-bar branch follows the editor; IntelliJ's log has a root filter while its widget follows the editor. Making focus the global scope stays open as its own task; it is a refactor of the six `pickRepo` copies, not a side effect of adding a view.

**Corrections to the owner's reading of VS Code, checked against the 1.135 manifest rather than memory.** The two screenshots are two different views: `CHANGES` with expandable repository rows is the **Source Control** view, while the click-to-select semantics belong to **Source Control Repositories** (`workbench.scm.repositories`), whose selection is *multi* and is a visibility filter. The `Pull · Push · Clone · Checkout to… · Fetch · Commit▸…` menu is `scm/repository` — the row's `⋯` button, 14 entries; **right-click** gives `scm/sourceControl`, which is *Close Repository*, *Close Other Repositories* and three worktree rows. Ours deliberately puts the verbs on right-click. And VS Code's own `scm.repositories.sortOrder` defaults to `discovery time`, not `name`; alphabetical is the owner's call and is what ships.

**What is shared rather than restated**, because a fourth surface over one registry is POST-P14d: rows carry `branchActions.branchContextValue` (extracted from `branchesView`, now read by both trees), the `Actions` rows are literally `HUB_VERBS`, the sections come from `branchHubModel.hubSections` lifted out of the popup's `buildPopupItems`, drag-and-drop dispatches into the legacy tree's existing `showDropActions`, and the root-naming rule (a colliding basename grows its parent) moved out of `LogViewProvider` into `core/util/repoLabels` now that three surfaces list the same roots. **One thing is deliberately better than the popup:** `feature/` is a real expandable node, so **D5** — which exists because a `QuickPick` cannot nest — does not apply to this surface.

**Two details worth naming.** The `Actions` verbs are tree rows, not title-bar buttons: a `view/title` button carries no argument, so it would fall back to the active editor's repository — the one repository this view may not be showing. The single exception, the `+` in the title strip, got its own `gittree.branches.newHere` for exactly that reason. And the focused row stays *selected* (`TreeView.reveal`), because a `TreeItem` cannot colour itself; the bold label is what survives the view losing focus.

Testing: **+27 unit** across three new files — `repoLabels` (collision, sort, stability), `repoScope` (first-row default, sticky pick, survives its repository leaving and returning, never unfocuses everything, fires only when the *resolved* root moved), `branchHubModel` (section order and membership, the checked-out branch never listed twice, favourites not repeated in Recent, real nesting, remote sections starting below their remote name). **+3 unit** in `branchMenu.test.ts`, including the one that matters: every branch-row `when` clause must name **both** view ids. It was watched to fail — reverting a single clause to `view == gittree.branches` turns it red — which is the class of defect POST-P14d shipped for years, invisible because a row that vanishes fails nothing. **+9 integration** (`sidebarSections.test.ts`) over the live views: one row per repository sorted by label, rows that do not expand, a row's command carrying its own root, exactly one focus that cannot be none, focusing moving the Branches view, Branches showing *only* the focused repository's refs with every row carrying that repository, verbs carrying the focused root, contextValues matching the shared clauses, and a nested branch rendering as folder → folder → leaf-segment. One of the nine drives the **real view** rather than the provider. The Repositories tree's element is the repository root *string*, so that VS Code hands every command in the row's context menu exactly the argument it already takes; `TreeView.reveal` is what proves that choice survives VS Code's own element↔handle map, which a provider-level test cannot reach. **One real defect caught while writing it:** the row command was first called `gittree.repositories.focus`, which VS Code already auto-registers for every contributed view — it is the command that reveals this very section, and the one the branches popup's last header button calls for `gittree.branches`. It is now `gittree.repositories.select`; nothing in the ext-host registry may squat on a `<viewId>.focus`. `phase14`'s view-set and POST-P14n gate assertions were updated rather than worked around: the list gained two ids, the gate now names `gittree.branchesLegacy`, and the "only `gittree.commit` opens unconditionally" invariant became "only `gittree.commit` and `gittree.branches`", with the Cursor reason written next to it.

Gates (scoped, per the owner's standing instruction): typecheck (both tsconfigs) + lint clean, **27 unit** on the new model files, **15 unit** on the two manifest-derived gates (`branchMenu`, `settingsDoc`), **65 integration passing / 0 failing** across `POST-P14u sidebar sections`, `P2 branches`, `P14 branch menu parity`, `P14 owning the SCM surface`, `Log roots & path filter` and `POST-P14p project-level branches popup`.

**Left open and reported:** whether focus should become the global repository scope (the `pickRepo` refactor above); `gittree.panel.title` is still `"Git"` rather than something branded; and the `p1-side` flake from POST-P14t is unexamined — `P1 core SCM` was not in this task's grep, so this run says nothing about it either way.

POST-P14v ✅ 2026-09-21 — **`Add to VCS` on a Ctrl-clicked set of unversioned files.** Owner request, straight out of IntelliJ: select several rows under *Unversioned Files*, right-click, and the files become versioned in the **default** changelist.

Two things were missing, and only one of them was the command. The commit tree's native context payload described **one row** — `{repoRoot, relPath}` — so every file verb it hosts (diff, open, rollback, move-to-changelist) silently acted on the row under the cursor and ignored the rest of the selection. `fileRowContext` now carries `relPaths` as well, built from the selection **as it stood before the right-click**: VS Code reads `data-vscode-context` off the DOM during that same contextmenu event, so a payload assembled from a selection the click itself changes would be one render behind. A click inside a multi-selection therefore carries the whole selection; a click outside it carries the clicked row, which is also what the tree then selects (IntelliJ's rule, new here — `FileTree` had no contextmenu handling at all).

`gittree.addToVcs` runs `git add -N`, not a plain `git add`. Intent-to-add makes git track the path without copying its content into the index, which is the invariant the changelists paradigm and per-change inclusion both rest on — `commitChangelistPartial` assembles its own tree in a scratch index and a pre-staged blob would fight it. Status still reads `A`, so the row turns green exactly as IntelliJ draws it. The list placement is explicit: `reconcile` adopts new tracked files into the **active** list, and IntelliJ's rule is the **default** one, so the command calls `moveFiles(…, DEFAULT_LIST_ID)` itself rather than letting adoption decide. The menu entry is gated on `fileUnversioned`, which is true only when *every* selected row is untracked — a mixed selection would half-apply — and the command re-filters on `isUntrackedPath` because a status refresh can land while the menu is open.
